ZADIES' LETTER. MY Dims NELLIE.

Since last week I have been tha fortunate recipient of a new blouse, which someone has very kindly presented me with. And it is such a lovely one too. If you could but see it on I am sure you would like it, because it fits so splendidly. Let me try and describe it to you. It is composed of 'cerise glace silk, and the body part is veiled with transparent sequin net. The latter need not necessarily be black, but that is the colour of mine. Gathered cerise chiffon compose the neck and collar. The blouse has cross-over revers, which extend into a turn-down collar at the back. 'The revers are covered alternately with frills, ,e.erise chiffon and kilted lisse. If you think you would like a blouse made after the same pattern, I should recommend you to get the lisse of the same colour as the net. The sleeves are plain, with lace at the cuffs, and the belt is composed of black velvet ribbon. Altogether I think you will agree that the foregoing will make a very dainty blouse. •

their cal _ man. coming winter season are just now occupying the minds of many mothers. I think the costumes are coming into vogue, and I think that you will agree with me that these garments 8110Uld be of a light, warm, yet durable substance. Some people of my acquaintance prefer a waterproof garment, but I have heard two opinions about them for winter wear. I should advise you to have a good look round before deciding the material of which the children's jackets are to be composed. But above all, although tastes in this direction differ, 7 should suggest that the capes be made plain.

Coiffures again,' imagine you to say Well, it is about this subject that I again address you. Another suggestion for the manner in which you may carry your hair. You know I don't like to bother you with so many different patterns that I have seen, but I trust you will forgive me for endeavouring to inform you as to the latest mode of coiffures, for you know how absurd it looks for a girl to appear a month behind time, or rather, fashion. This time it is the evening coiffure. The hair should be slightly undulated in wide, natural waves, which do not suggest the curling irons. You will -require two frames or frizzettes, one to cross the top of the head and taper off above the ears, and the other to go below the head. These are fixed after the lower part of the hack hair is waved and combed down, and the front from the ears upwards is allowed to fall over the face. The hair is then turned up at the back, and all the front drawn over the top frizette, and the ends collected and securely tied. If not sufficient for the knot, they may be pinned fiat, and a few curls made from the ends, and a knot may be fixed or and upheld by a jewelled comb. A few ends n...y be rled and brought to the front, a narrow comb with paste top having a very graceful effect in front of the coil. You will lid this coiffure is not at all difficult to arrange, more especially if the hair is thick, but short, when the small knots may be added, and the ends uted for the little fluffy

1 daresay you will be thinking of presenting books to some of your little friends. Every winter the number of iitory-boolts especially intended for the delectation of young people seems to increase. Although "a sift-book" must necessarily present a handsome appearance I would remind you that any intelligent boy or girl would rather have a really good story in the plainest ot covers than a tedious tale beautifully bound. That old absurd notion that anything in the way of literature would do for young people, provided that it was quite innocuous end pointed a "moral," has Almost expired. - -

I have been looking at vast numbers of hats -during the last week, hut the best I think I have seen was a grey ribbed felt. On the right side there were two large ostrich feathers of a greyish tinge, nearly resemblitig the hat in colour, whilst • coming from under the brim was a similar feather, which partially covered tme brim. In front of a low crown was placed a black bow of velvet. I - saw also a similar hat, with brim bending down- - wards back and front, and in a golden brown felt, had a big flai, bow of dark brown velvet across the brim in front, behind which a band of sable was so arranged as to cover almost entirely the brim at the back. The very smartest of toques is nothing but a close-fitting shape plainly covered with • velvet, and two ostrich feathers curling round the brim. I should suggest as the only other adorn. meat, a heavy gold buckle placed in front so as to hide the joining of the two feathers. Your loving

WOMEN'S CHAT." Although the boar's head for the Queen's Christmas boat dis generally supplied by her Imperial grandson in Germany, ahe stands hi little need of such a gift—as ber Majesty possesses a fine herd at her own. The breed was good ia the days of George IV, and the Prince Con- gers greatly inipeovect it, and enlarged the enclosure where the evil I Swine are kept. Eecelleet epecirnens of the saugher are the mutt, and a favourite walk of the Royal guests at Windsor is in the direction of the Schweingarten where the great beasts ext,and fight,and bask tneir days away. The beef laid on the Royal table is, of course, supplied from beasts on Her Maje,ty's own farms, they being among the finest in the ceuretry, and fully deserving the numerous paz,..s they carry off during the year. A con- trioutiou of beef is also sent from Sandringham, for the Preece of Wales, thorough country gentleman and Practicil termer as he is, stands second only to his Royal mether in this dirmion,and often makes presents of barons at beef, at the great winter feast, to his relatives. Preparations for Christmas entail an enormous amount of work on the Royal kitchen at Madrid, which is con- trailed by a Frenchman. He has at his orders, more than thirty individuals who are divided into cooks, but- lers, ice confectioners, pastry cooks, assistant cooks, and se:unions. The style of cooking is French, with a con- siderable admixture Di Spaniela and German dishes. . Dinner is partaken of at seven o'clock, and when this meal is over, the lahoers of the French head cook, and meet of his suboreinates come to an end. The servants doing night duty then teens their work, if such it may be celled, since it consists of sitting up all night in case any member of the Royal Family desires anything in the eating and driukiag way—a thing which occurs very seldem. The Princess of Wales' autograph book is a huge volume, and one in which Her Royal Highness takes a keen interest. It contains the name of almost evsry celebrity in the world, and its leaves many of them, Kaye been 'ruminated by the Marchinness of Lorne, the Empress Fiederick and several ether members of the Royal Feintly. Lord Ki,emeuer, tefors leaving Sandring- ham was b, n Ailed Yek,ii a request that lie would inscribe Lite name theeem. Compared with many men of fame, Lord Kitchener write an excellent hand, as dess also Lord Wolseley. The Price° of Wales writes fairly well, a h gible, rather heavy hand, with a Sant to cue right, and a freedom Item flourish. Lord Salisbury can scarcely be said to excel to this particular uirecti ;n. The Premier writes with a tine nib, lightly, and very badly. Mr. A. J. Baifour takes utter his uncle, les writing being very difficialt to oecipher. Leidy Jerkey, wh with the Earl, is entertaining a suc- eeteien of gm sts at Middleton Park, near Bicester, is a woman of rare social tact and mental gifts, and these give her a more enduring title, as Queen of Scciete„ than mere pescmal charms can furnish. Her three daughters " divinely tall, and most divinely tair " inheris, their mother's secial gifts, and unaffected cearm of manner. Lady Jersey has written a good deal, her bast work having been done for children, for whom she has written a play, several stories, and two volumes of hymns. During theitwo years her husband was Governor of New South Wales, Lady Jersy was hie right hand, and immensely popular. The latest engagement rings are beine Let with single stones, and these,usually diamonds. When Her Majesty was in her twenties, and until a much later period, the only stone recognised as fashionable for engagement rings was tee diamond. Then came the opal, but the superstition connected with that stone interfered consi- derably with its prosperity, and it was Boon banished in favour of the turquoiee, the blue of which, is supposed to he symbolic of happy days. The Empress Eug6aie favoured emeralds, and as she was recognized as ths leader of fashion all over Eurepe, the vogue enjoyed by the incomparable green stone was very great. Then followed she reign of sapphires which lasted more than a decade, text came the ruby, and now we have the diamond again. Diamonds in conslderable numbers are still being found in New South Wales, but the true matrix has not yet been eiscoveted. When both its lociale and nature have been ascertained, it is probable that New South Wales will become one of the lesdiag diamond producing countries u the world. The work of prospecting is i how ever el fficult, tedious, and costly, by reason of the mountainous character of the parts in which gems are plincirally found. Aitlficial pearls are produced in abundance, so are diamonds, rubies, and emeralds, which are turned out in the labera'ory, taut they ate of little value. There is thouele a new system for the cultivation of pearls which, as now produced, cannot be distinguished from the real and genuine article. This consists of introducing 601130 sinag foreign substance into the shell of the pearl oyeter, in order that the mollusc, to relieve iteelf front the irritation so caused, may elver it with nacre. The practiced introducing grains of sand into the shell has long been known in the East, tut the oyster can nearly always eject them, and if net the result would only be a foreign eutstance coated with pearl. The new method is to intro :ute a small, trod comparatively valueless pearl,weieh is done by holing the shell, and securing it ineide to the great inconvenience cf the oyster. In about two years, time, if all goes well, this is trans- formed into a fine large specimen, which dealers cannot tell Irons the true orient pearl. Boas are altering their shape somewhat. The flat, or round vatiete are no longer emaned by fashionable *omen. Cp-to-clate boas show a decided inclination to warde the Medici collar, and stand away from the face. The mode is a becoming ens, and those women already in poisession c,i furs which bays been used as Medici collar trimmings should lose no time in convert- in.; them into a boa, when ehey will have the satiefac- t: on of knowing that they arc "moving with the times " and at ,i.tle experse. The fans being shown just now are, many of them lovely, almost teyond description. There are the white ostrich feather specimens, with a handsome aigrette, and mounted on mother o'pearl stieks,tnen there are the real white lace fans, with gauze centre piece, painted ia a design of cherub' and fairies, mounted on mother o'pearl sticks, inlaid with gold. For those who prefer, from choice Of necessity, to be more economical, there are white gauze fans, lightly spangled with steel sequins white silk fans with band painted medallions, in the Watt( an style, or black net specimens, ornamented with appliques of white lace. The most stately of all, are undoubtedly, thoee built of ostrich leathers. The Prin- cess ef Wales, who, with the Marchioness of Lorne, and the Duchess of York, is an ardent fan admirer prefers this style beyond all others. Tremendously thgioccleoutiteenrcr flannels, overinge anond a crn ot koeeppatrhae. body warm as do d w tivrey light costume can safely be worn if the under- clothing is of some warm material. Cellular cloth is ex- cellent for urderwear, it washes well and does not need to be ironed. For night dresses it is invaluable, and ean be had in any variety of shades, stripes and pretty pale tones. There are two qualities of cellular cloth, the bet- ter having an admixture of silk in it. which makes it extra warm and comfortable. Scone Pudding.—Put two eges on the part of the eeales where the weights are usually placed, then weigh against them dour, castor eugar,and butter, so that you will get the exact weight of the eggs in each. Now put the butter and sugar into a basin, and work it until soft and creamy. Stir in the yolks of the two eggs (well betatni), add the flour, and half a teaspoonful of baking powder, also about six dreier of vanilla essence. Whisk the whites of the eggs to a stiff froth,aad stir lightly in. Well butter a mould or basic, reur in the mixture, and bake in a quick oven 30 minutes. Turn out on a hot dish, and serve with plain boiled custard, jam, or timed fruit It is delicious hot or cold. " MADGE."

rHE EXAMINER FRIDAY, DECEMBER 16 1898

(411 rights reserved.) THE

Yrincess, a trusty friend hath taken hilt. .lastward that he may discover thy husband and

Sandy Dees, to tell them of thy conditioa, urging them hither with speed." "This is thoughtful of thee, Pecos, and true friendship—for, unlike many who profess friend- ship, thou dost not forsake me in my adversity." And then they went within the house, and conned over recent events until the eventide sped.

But still the people surged along the shore, and anon word was brought that a revolution was in full swing, forced to a head by the discovery that certain high officials harboured the Queen and designed to set Olive at liberty. Alas! the infec- tion spread even to the guards surrounding the lake prison. The awful seriousness of this was soon apparent. But Olive looked calm and resigned, as though she beheld some glory beyond this turmoil.

At nightfall the Royal prisoner noticed that island was floating with the breeze in the directior, of the shore—an evidence that the cables had 1).--,en

_ cut from the moorings. They could hear i. babble of thousands of voices, and the women's shrieks of ap;flause. Then the courage of Olive asserted itself, for, taking the hand of Pecos, she calmly said: "See, this people clamour for my body, to deliver it into the hands of the tor- mentors. If I come not out of this with my life, and thou art preserved, tell Mark that my love never faltered. The very guards set to watch over me, have betrayed their trust. Furthermore, as thou dost see, a rope is made fast, and willing hands pull us towards the people who madly howl for their prey. 0 God, Thy will be done! " And the soul of Pecos groaned for his lady; but amid such a frantic multitude one man's help was of no avail. Ile shuddered as he saw the light of many torches shimmering upon the rippling tide. He put his fingers in his ears to shut out the horrible imprecations of Goya's desperadoes. The strange prison floated nearer the expectant multitude. Then Olive saw that the High Priest's aim was frustrated, and that her treacherous guards were undoubted!' a party to this new stratagem, for they formed themselves along the beach ready to receive her. Their object was evident, for wild cries rent the air: "Throw her to the beasts!" This summons smote Olive with dismay. Presently some torch-bearers, headed by Goya, stepped upon the island, and approached their Viet im. Their fierce eyes and barbarous manners struck terror into the heart of this lonely half- caste, and for a moment she leant upon Pecos fc: support, her lips blanclAd and her body quivering. "Save that Mark may even now be coming," she murmured, "I would make an end of it in these waters." _ . _

"The life that the Great Spirit gave, thou hast no right to take away," urged the faithful Pecos. "Courage, Princess ; it may yet be well with thee." Further conversation was interrupted by the hands of the foremost guards, for they seized the Princess and dragged her through the multi- tude. Never had Pecos seen so weird a sight, but in the people's estimation he was a worm fit only to be trodden to death—a result which had surely, happened save that when the mutineers kicked and hit him he slipped aside and was lost in the swaying crowd. 'While a graceful fawn was being hounded down, the deformed Pecos was considered beneath contempt. So, finding himself of no account and helpless to save the Princess, he made haste to precede her, and contrived to inform some friendly-disposed counsellors of the people's threats, and how ronghly they were likely to handle her on the way. Olive's friends could only shake their heads and deplore the madness of their kinsmen. The High Priest himself had fared the fate of liana, and was a prisoner in his own magnote, or Temple. Evidently Goya and his revengeful followers had carefully planned this revolt, for even the lions had been removed from the Royal menagerie and placed in the dens approaching the amphitheatre arena. The brutal nature of this Indian race had conquered, and all the discipline of centuries had failed to stamp out barbarous superstition. The more gentle and heroic their victim, the greater their eagerness to destroy. Does the tame lion forget how to lap blood? So evil-disposed men, armed cap-a-pie, lighted resin-wood torches. Many women had suddenly lost all sense of decent behaviour, and in their wild delirium some fainted and were trampled to death. Mothers stood at their windows, and holding up their little children, taught them how to mock the royal lady. Satan was stirring men up to mutiny. The imprecations were bad enough, but when drums were beaten and the trumpets blared, and the.procession looked like a horde let loose from Pandemonium, then the lamb that was being led to the slaughter fell exhausted by the way. The commotion now reached a dangerous pitch, for when some saw that Olive's face was placid and death-like they howled with rage. However, a curricle was brought, into which Goya, the strong man, lifted the Princess, and when her swoon was passed the multitude gave a shout of exultation, pressing forward with even greater haste. As the people were almost as exclusive as the Chinese and Japs of ancient times, not a single white man was there to witness this pandering to brutality. All the State leaders were incarcerated by a preconcerted plan, and such of the military ar wished to shield their Princess were forced to revile the desecration of her ancestral dead in order to mingle with the escort and keep back the people. But their silence or sympathy was of little avail against the machinations of a superstitious horde, whose fanaticism now destroyed all sense of shame and smothered all feelings of humanity. Natural instincts may be subdued, but they cannot be blotted out. Once a redskin always a redskin. And so it befell that Olive was hurried into tip amphitheatre while the people crowded the tier& A bright moon shone down upon this weird' scene —shone down upon the Princess as she knelt upon the ground and clasped her hands in prayer. Those who looked upon her face could see no sign of fear, not even when the torches blazed and her merciless keepers left her alone in the arena and proceeded to unbolt the doors of the dens. She rose from her knees when the' beasts bounded forth, and the whole multitude ceased its clamour during a minute of triumphant expectation. The sight- seers strained their necks to catch the earliest glimpse of the first beast which should spring upon this young wife of an absent husband. Every square foot of room was occupied, and thousands of the disappointed stood without waiting for the shouts which should proclaim that the moment of retribution had come—as a surging crowd of white men and women, remember, wait Without a gaol to watch for the hoisting of the black flag—as a multi- tude of civilised Europeans have gloried in the death throes of men and beasts. There were some, however, who shuddered when they saw the fate in store for Olive, and yet a few who wept bitterly. But when it came to pass that the victim boldly walked towards the angry beasts, even her enemies were, loud in praise of her courage. "Behold I " they cried, "she goes boldly to her death. She hath the soul of a god!" Presently a strange, hushed silence fell upon that concourse—a lull so significant that some dropped dead with suppressed excitement, or, more properly speaking, from a failure of the heart's action ;for, as Olive courageously faced the animals they immediately became as listless as beasts in a travelling menagerie on the approach of their tamer. The people stared with amazement, refusing to believe their senses until several lions prowled around the arena, those near the Princess neither harming her nor showing any evidences of their natural brutality. One or two crouched beside her as though guarding her from harm, and she patted and fondled them as a child might pet dogs. At this unexpected turn Goya and his nefarious crew howled themselves hoarse with rage, some throwing burning torches at the nearest beasts to goad them to anger. But they listlessly sought the centre,of the arena about the Princess, and she had no difficulty in taming those that roared at the spectators as if in defiance. Seeing this apparently marvellous power possessed by Olive, her frit'''. raised the appropriate cry: "The good spirits their royal ancestors defend her!" Then came that sudden revulsion of feeling so common with superstitious fanatics, and the people began to cry'aloud : "She is one of the gods!" "Her death would be our destruction! " and the

like: Now for a space of two minutes or more, Goya and the leaders of the faction argued among them- selves, for they would save their own skins by releas- ing the Princess from her predicament. Shortly the trumpeters sounded the call for silence, and amid a profound hush Goya cried with a loud voice from the vicinity of the arena gates : "Come hither, Princess, thou art free!" Now Olive stood as one dazed, as though doubt- ing whether she was not safer in the arena, than outside among her kinsmen. So the summons was repeated still more urgently, and the cry of freedom was raised on all sides, for many hearts beat with feverish anxiety for their Princess. Then She comprehended that the multitude cried not with mockery, and she calmly strode forward, anon stopping and facing the now restless beasts. By such careful means she at length reached the Cates, log one snarling animal followed her. Those

of her mem! wno wisnea to maintain ner prestige as a demi-god suggested that, in order to prevent the beasts evening. lont- thin poles should b brought and lighted torches tied to them. The suspense for Olive while this was done, passes understanding; only one placed in a similar pre- dicament could realise it. _ _

At length half-a-score mounted the gates and covered her retreat by brandishing the fire, scaring the animals, and enabling her to pass out unharmed. - Then a hundred, willing hands were ready to assist her; but Olive needed none of this, for her will-power was great, even as her faith in r'-ld was steadfast. True, after a while she ~.,.ggered as one drunk with wine, but, knowing ..:er own courage alone would now gain her liberty, zhe fought down a sickly feeling which threatened to overpower her. Her brave demeanour won further admiration, and the people crowded to do her service, as eagerly as a few hours ago they howled for her blood. As for the conspirator Goya, he was seized by the multitude, and amid the execrations of the women, he was hissed, cursed, and beaten out of the city. His charm was torn from his breast, and his utter excommunication pronounced by the High Priest. Such are the unreasonable phases of the human mind under the tension of abnormal excitement.

Seeing Pecos struggling to reach her, and a chariot drawing up for her use, she made a path for the hunchback, and he ran by the roan's side.— his eyes IWwith joy, his heart full of thankfulness, his own aches and pains all forgotten in his enthu- siasm over his lady's deliverance. The instincts of this deformed retainer ,irere singularly generous, reminding one of the days of British chivalry when servants sacrificed their lives for those they were proud to serve. "Whither, Princess? " shouted Pecos in her ear, for the cheers were deafening. "To my mother," answered Olive, evidently judging that the turn of events in her own favour might enable her to save Kane and her counsellors. Solicitude for the welfare of a good parent is the surest sign of a child's kindly disposition. Olive loved her mother. So sturdy friends piloted the vehicli unto the royal palace, the multitude singing (.riumphal songs, and demonstrating by much clamour hew easily a mob can be moved to good works by mere impulse, or goaded to evil deeds by unprincipled agitators.

CHAPTER XXIV.

Trill FUGITIVES IN DANGER.

The meeting between Kana and Olive was veil affecting, but the Queen knew the fickle nature of mob popularity, and subdued her motherly feelings to the exigencies of public clamour. The crowd which halted without the• palace, cheered and shouted so incessantly that both the royal ladies bravely struggled against painful anxiety, and lamps were ordered to be hung outside, that the people might see mother and daughter bow their acknowledgments. Then Kana put her arm round Olive's neck, and without more ado, the two went out upon the balcony. Their appearance was the signal for a mighty shout of curiosity rather than joy. One low-bred fellow hissed, but he was struck on the mouth, his face scratched, and his woven overall torn to shreds by women. This episode produced a greater ovation, madcaps and fools, hare-brained chatterers and shrivelled hags vying for superiority of hideous clamour. Staid men waved their caps, soldiers their metal helmets, modest women their handkerchiefs. A sickly man who, out of sheer weakness, slipped to the ground, was well-nigh trampled to death. Snatches of patriotic songs were sung, and between each new tune those intoxicated with excitement shrieked and shouted themselves hoarse. One steady onlooker was heartily jeered for not shewing keener manifesta- tion of joy. Young bloods fought for place, egged on by girls who had suddenly lost all sense of self- respect. It was a veritable babel for the space of five minutes. Then Kana seized the opportunity of a lull to step back a pace, and Olive saluted the multitude three times. In return the seething mass made a trinity of complimentary signs, raising the right hand for Power, touching the forehead for Wisdom, and kissing the fingers for Love. The High Priest then came forward, and raising two fingers, secured sufficient silence to call for a "Cheer for Princess Olive." Flattered by this request, Olive smiled as the people cheered; but her face was white, and she held to the balustrade as though she would faint with exhaustion. Then Kana came forward dignified and calm. She bowed in stately fashion, and as the lights from the lamps around and the torches below shone upon her intelligent face, those near noticed the wintry smile which momentarily chased away her anxious looks.

The populace having shewn the full extent of their frenzy and bawled themselves as hoarse as mallards, began to fall away. Then Kana took Olive by the hand, and together they went in, leaving the packed mass below to disperse when they were tired—for the Court officers dare not command, lest a tumult should result and jeopardise the throne. No tongue can tell the ordeal Kana had passed through during the last forty-eight hours; only mothers can realise her anxiety as to her daughter's future, for Olive was not yet out of danger. Immediately the balcony windows were closed and the blinds drawn the Queen again clasped the Princess to her bosom, and the two wept aloud for joy. Yet there was a -touch of sadness, for both trembled, wondering how long this respite would last. "Listen, Olive, the people thought a miracle was wrought, and have exalted thee into a goddess. Thou art not so silly as to believe this. We mixed a pungent juice with the beasts' drink,which lulled their brute nature." "Some of the animals, mother, I reared, and all I daily fed. We were quite friends.", "Go thou at once, Olive, after thine husband, lest a revulsion of feeling come about, and the beasts' appetites be keen. Let us prepare for thy departure at once, and Pecos with thee, for this I vowed to thine husband before he would consent to flee without thee." Olive prepared herself for travelling with mat haste, and Kana gathered such jewels and precious stones as could be carried in a small compass. The Princess went forth with Pecos by the same route That Mark had taken. This last farewell between bother and daughter was most heartrending, for something told Kane that evil overshadowed her kingdom, and that she would never again see her only child. In the dead of the night Pecos led the royal lady by a lonely trail that struck right into the heart of the mountains. They travelled with much labour and no little hazard till noon of the next day, when Pecos pointed to the bossy cumuli and shrugged his shoulders. The clouds were broken arid massive, as if the whole watershed of Golden Vale were transplanted in the heavens. There were canons and magnificent waterfalls, side gorges and mighty peaks of dazzling light, and dark patches like forests. Within an hour zig-zag forks of lightning darted through the firs in quick succes- sion, and these were soon followed by thunder, whose reports were as loud as great explosions of guncotton. Crash ' went a flash, right into the heart of a cluster of firs, some of which were shattered from top to bottom. Their splitting into long rails and slivers made Olive tremble, but the faithful retainer shielded her as best he was able. The two escaped harm, and being somewhat pre. pared for the deluge which followed, they saw without much danger the rare sight of the ground covered with water like a shimmering carpet. Out of the bosom of the clouds poured showers, giving life and beauty to the landscape below • from the ••!.:Ist of the thunder they sped to join the roar of Irsaracts, or to beat into finer spray the splashing iiwers of the mighty cascades. 7ecos, let us press onward, for I fear betrayal." The fugitives slept mainly when the sun was high, and the heat oppressive. For shelter by night Pecos made two tents of branches covered with brushwood and leaves interwoven. They recognised the "tokens " left by Mark. On the fourth morning suddenly the ground swayed like a canoe gently rocked. Looking back towards the Golden Vale they were astonished to see a tall column of black smoke rise into the sky like a great pine tree. They were greatly alarmed, for the crown spread over the earth until a strange darkness gathered about them, and the air was stifling hot. Olive extended her arms and clasped her hands in prayer, but Pecos took a more practi- cal view of the danger and urged the Princess onward. Their skin became frightfully dry, their lips parched, and their tongues seemed to cleave to the roof of their mouths. "I have a strange sensation of tightness about my throat," hoarsely said Olive, "as if the muscles were contracting. God help us! " The odour gave Pecos a nasty hacking cougt one moment he sweated, and then his skin was I though dried before a huge fire. Soon Olive feh sick and faint, and he just managed to catch her as she fell by the way. • The heavens rained fire- dust, and though he was suffering acute agony ye, the faithful Pecos mustered au all his streusti

and dragged the I'nm:us beneath a thick -elustei of trees. He placed her by a brooklet, which came clear and refreshing from a rock. Cast down by his lady s anguish, Pecos invoked the Gres. Spirit's aid. His prayer was heard, for Olive soon recovered, and together they trudged on, suffering ' much physical pain, for the hot ground blistered and scorched the soles of their feet. The cinder- dust spread like a carpet for many miles. At last Pecos cried joyfully : "Look, my lady, the devil's cloud clears!" . Their food was exhausted, but they hopefully struggled on. The rumblings towards Golden Vale - had ceased, but when the light about them waxed stronger they still saw a mighty cloud hanging to , the westward, black as soot, through which forked ' lightning flashed. "Pecos," gasped Olive, for her throat was parched, "the Great Spirit is angry. If we stop i we starve; let us press on eastward where the air is l less thick." And they walked for a mile over 3 country strewn with fiery cinders which had blotted out vegetation. Every step added to their misery; the very birds dropped dead at their feet. It seemed as if the smoke would choke all that breathed, but they covered their mouths with damp cloths, and suffer- ing much at last reached the edge of the cloud. Hunger now gnawed, and soon extreme thirst drove them half mad( for the few streams tasted bitter and made them vomit. "Hunger is misery enough," plaintively whispered Olive, "but this thirst is torture. 0 Mark, my love, why tarry so long! Come quickly, or I die! ' Soon her strength waned, and by nightfall Pecos saw that the great physical strain and mental , anguish during that terrible journey had com- pletely prostrated the Royal lady. They were in a : "hanging meadow" of the sub-alpine district. It ' extended from above the timber line to the bottom ,•, of a lake basin, in contour like cascades broken ~ here and there by boulders, and nursing a gurgling 1 stream down its centre. It was the home of beavers, the soil was coarse and boggy, and the vege- tation rank. In this lonely mountain nest Pecos Speedily lit a fire and made things as comfortable for the Princess as circumstances permitted. But all his tender care would be of no avail unless succour speedily came. He spread a blanket by the fire, and placed the sufferer upon it, covering her with his garments and moistening her lips and ' bathing her feverish brow. As he watched, with the solicitude of the tenderest nurse, and when his ' hope of her recovery was well-nigh spent, his quick ears caught the sound of low voices. Peering into the darkness, he could just see that strangers were cautiously approaching. He tested their 't friendship by uttering a labial sound used by Kana's hunters, and his heart leaped with joy when a response came which admitted of no decep- tion. A familiar voice rang out upon the mid- night air: "Sandy, here is Pecos, kneeling by the side of a sick woman!" "God be praised!" cried Pecos, tears of thank' fulness streaming down his dusky face. "It is her good lord, and my master!" Mark rushed up, and recognising the prostrate form, exclaimed, as one terrified: "My God, it le my wife 1 " He kissed her, fondled her, called her i by name, and presently her eyes opened and she ~,, looked fondly at him, a senile flitting across her . sickly face. . Then her lips moved, but she was unable to articulate. "The fountain of life had dried up, save for thine arrival," said Pecos. "Warm bear's fat will , revive her; it is as a shower to the parched grass." It was a labour of love and duty for us to tend the Princess in her extremity, and Mark heaved a , sigh of relief when she revived sufficiently to take -, / a little warm nourishment. Olive had pined for - her husband, and his coming seemed to awaken an interest in life which had almost fled. She brightened when he kissed her, and tenderly bathed her burning brow; but so great was her prostration that she was unable even to raise her- self on her elbow. He gently rubbed the palms of her hands, and after a time of great anxiety she sufficiently recovered to put her arms around his neck. Tears of thankfulness relieved her mind, and her strength gradually returned. "Her predicament, wi'oot protection," I whis-.; pered, "is some mad freak; or dire necessity drovw her to't." Mark was puzzled; but when she slept Pecos told us the mournful story. "Pecos," said my chief, "thou hest won my' life-long gratitude—a poor and hollow tribute-- with Mum seeking to murder me!" . 4,4,• The pathetic situation added significance to this - ...Y declaration, and I siglied. Pecos modestly re- sponded that his anxiety and hard work were amply repaid by the deliverance of his Princess. Mark's rank imposed obligation, but Pecos'' deformity did not rob him of nobility of soul. "Such adversity has overtaken us," said my chief resignedly, "that will neod all our courage. Thank God, Olive is on the mend." "Cheer up I " said I • "a man oft begins t' shine when doon on his luck:" Pecos and I rigged up a rough-and-ready tent, while Mark watched. Our success surprised and pleased him, and he thanked us; for a word of praise in due season puts heart into a man. Our * improvised shelter protected Olive from the chilly air. ' Her, good constitution enabled her to re- cuperate speedily. But her convalescence detained us in that home of orange lilies, larkspurs, and many species of mimulus and pentstemon for three days. We were fully seven thousand feet above the Pacific, and yet this meadow-girdle was bril- liantly illumined with rich flowers. We could see down the valley picturesque bogs with deep growth of yellow and brown sphagnum, handsome shades of green and delightful patches of kalmia ripening into the tints of autumn, interesting in variety and enticing to us weary travellers as we wended our • way towards the Golden Vale to ascertain the fate of Kana and her people. The first night-frosts were just beginning W ~ 'sting; the leaves of the heathworts looked a rich A purple in the alpen-glow, and the slightly bronzed r grasses harmoniously blended in colour with the ' few golden-rods and daisies that continued to bloom. The frost diamonds on the grass panicles and leaves radiated the early morning sunbeams, forming dazzling colours to vie with those of the late butterflies which came forth as the suu mounted the heavens and gave an unsurpassed brilliancy to the landscape. , Alas! the scene suddenly changed. The land before us was blackened by the showers of scorile, stones, and pumice. The approaches to Golden Vale looked as though a dirty smoking blanket had been spread over everything. Here at least the graceful mazes of ths 'lance would never charm the sight; the young malitens, wreathed with flowers , and lightly clad in classic garments, would never more issue forth for healthy amusement. , With many heartburnings we picked our waY., along hazardous crags, where the melted glacial snows had cooled the surface. Olive's minute knowledge of the paths saved us many a danger, ' but even then we dare not venture lower than the brows of stupendous cliffs, making our way foot by k, foot to the highest pinnacle east of liana's strong- ,- hold. Sad to relate, our footsteps were somewhat guided by reason of many bodies we discovered along the outer trails—victims of the eruption, ; suffocated by nauseous vapours, and burnt berna ' recognition. Before me now in clear outline rises the figure of Princess Olive as she viewed the bodies of those struck down by the way, now lying in all postures, as though struggling with fate in the mead rush to escape. Shortly we were startled to hear a painful human , cry, and turning round saw a big Indain struggliq ,- along the side of a near ridge chased by a drove a . vicious Teccaries. The man's endurance was hausted, and the fearless brutes were worrying hill to death. His clothes were torn to shreds, an his flesh hacked and bruised in a most frightfu! manner. We were about to fire, but Olive stoPPeSI us, for the cannonading of a full battery W 00... have no more eFfect upon them than the poPPini of fire crackers. The wild hogs made short lor , of their victim, and we were thankful to them rush pell-mell into the valley, for had they -, scented us our chances of escape were poor. vcrl , advanced cautiously to examine their victim, aut. ' to our surprise discovered the mangled remains 01 ~ the leonine Goya! ~This is just punishment for a pal spy,cd " staid Mark. Olive took from Goya's reticule, that the beast' had trodden and torn, a tattered parchment, urrat , which was pictured writing, much finger-user ; and blood-stained. As she read, her face gre , terribly pale and her brow anxious. "This sets forth," she said tremulously, "the reward for capture; the plan to seize and immolate rue for um. desecration of the Royal dead; and the iastril!. . tions how thou wart to be tortured and barui . alive! OUR ALL'NttCK ILLUSTRA-

TION.

THE PROPOSED NITA' CONS VAlia L %AIX

BUILDING&

The sheet alcaanack which we preseut ts our readers to-day contains, as will be seen, a picture of the proposed new constabulary buildiags, police and coroner's court &c. for Warrington, as per the accepted design by Mr. It. Burns Dick, architect, lof Newcsetle-on-Tyne. The architect is now besy prepariug the specific itions for the work, and when these are ready, the Corporation will at once adver- tise for tenders for the erec ;ion of the buildings. It may therefore be presumed that early in toe twentieth csntury Warrington will be p )seteeted of police buildings, handsome in appearance and coms modious in every respect for the purposes for which they are intended, Such a fine block of buildings for constabulary purposes forms a striking contrast with those which served a somewhat similar pur- pose at the beginning of the present century now fast drawing to its close. Aided largely by the literary efforts of the late Mr. William Beatnont we of the present day are able to catch a glimpse of what the Warrington brtdewell was towards the beginning of the present century. It appears that it was then situated behind a large three-storied building in Church-street where the paupers were kept in constant work,either in spinning,weaving or Other work, and whose combined earnings seem to have been about six pounds a week.Joon Howard,the great reformer of prisons visited it whilst sojourn- ing in Warrington while printing some part of his works on the subject he bad made his own. His des- cription of it is interesting at the present juncture. He says This place consisted of two rooms, one of which was about nine feet square, with bedsteads and straw, but no window. The other room was about nine feet long and four feet wide. There was no employment, and the diet which was sent from the house was the same as the paupers', who judg- ing from their looks,seemed to be humanely treated. The officer lived in the house, but received no salary for his office." It is pleasant to know that the philanthropist saw no prisoner in this dreary place. The small size of the prison argues well for the rare occurrence of serious crime, for two prisoners at a time would have been too many for the prison to hold. But the measure of the prison and of the police were the same, for one hat covered the whole police force, except the Manor constables, whose office was almost honorary. Mr. Beamont records the fact that if serious cases were few fights and occasional uproars in the streets were plentiful. Street fights between men stripped to the skin were not only not unknown, but were not always stopped. It is amusing to read that the one police officer who looked after the peace of the town was so stout that he resembled the man in the song who seemed like " two single gentlemen rolled into one." lie could not have given effectual chase after or taken a runaway thief without hav- ing had such unexpected assistance as another officer, who, being seen by a bystander, engaged in an unequal thief-race, the latt-r gained in the chase, overtook the runaway, and jumping on his back, detained him till his panting pursuer came up. It was not until the passing of the Improve- ment and Market Act of 1854 that the Corporation were enabled to remove the bridewell to the pre- sent police station in Irlam-street. No buildings in the town have perhaps served their purpose more fully than these. But with the continued growth ,of the Borough and the increasing duties of the police it became absolutely necessary that more commodious premises should be obtained. The present premises have been condemned again and again by the Government Inspector as inadequate for the purpose, and a threat to withhold tie police grant left the Watch Committee with no other alternative than to provide more suitable premises. It was not until the early part of the present year that a site for new premises was determined upon Most of our readers will be aware that the pro- posed buildings as shown on the drawing are to ba eracte I upon the site new occupied by the militia barracks at the junction of Arpley-street and Wilson-Patten-street. It will be necessary to er- tirely clear the site, and the whole of the buildings now standing will shortly disappear. A few par- ticulars respecting the barracks will not be out of place. They were erected in the year 1872 and were occupied by the 4th Royal Lancashire Militia in 1873. They continued to be used for this pur- pose until the removal of the Militia to the new barracks at Orford, the headquarters of the brigade dep6t. Subsequently the premises were used for a short time as a school by the Guar- dians of the Warrineton Union for which they paid a rent of £lBO a year. The structure includes an armoury, clothing stores, hospital and living rooms, dwellings for thirteen families,orderly and officers rooms, pay and quarter- master's offices, guard rooms, cells, wash-houses, stables, drill shed, magazine, &c. rho whole was the property of the County Palatine of Lancaster, and contains 7,738 superficial square yards. It was offered for sale by public auction in April,lBB3, but was not sold. It was, however, afterwards secured for the Corperotion by the Property Pur- chasing Committee during the mayoralty of the late Alderman Greenall. The price paid was £4,750; 1 £5O of this sum being paid as commission to a local agent. For some years, a portion of the build- ings has been used in connection with the techni- cal instruction classes, and at present no arrange- ments have been made to provide another home for these classes. - . . .

eetimated cost of the new police buildings is £16,500. The style of architecture is not bleed upon any particular period of architectural history but it has been treated in what may be termed free ren- naisance adapted to suit the materials proposed to be used. A broad simplicity is aimed at. not only because of the limited cost, but because of it being more appropriate to the nature of the buildings. The various offices, courts, etc.. have been placed in positions most convenient to their special uses. The public entrance will be equally convenient for either of the courts and is close eo the constabulary offices. The staircase has been arranged with this object in view, the hall or public lobby of the Coroner's Court being directly on the left, and that of the Police Court to the right. Faltered from these halls are the witnesses' rooms for their respective courts; and the solicitors' rooms near the Police Court with the consultation room. between them and the witnesses' rooms will be quite convenient for either court. The entrance to public galleries in each case will be quits distinct from those leading to the body of the Court. The Police Court will be over the cells so that from the latter direct access will be obtainable to the dock. The Police Court will be so placed as to cause no discomfort flom external noise. The Coroner's Court being less frequently used will be placed to the front. The Magistrates' stairs will communicate with an upper hell from which their room, the Maeistrates' Clerk's, the Coroner's rooms and Bench of Police Court all opeu direct. The Magistrates' stair- case has also been so arranged as to afford A speedy and direct communication between the Coroner's Court and the doctor's room and mortuary with- out leaving the buillings. The Chief Constable's offices will be near the magistrates' entrance,which will also be used by the Watch Committee whoee room adjoins it. The detective department will be at the South-east corner near to the public entrance and midway between the Chief Constable's and the Chief Inspector's offices. Special attention has been given to the cells and police offices and parade room. The corridor and hall of the police offices will be'contiguous to the Parade room and the last named will be overlooked from the ser- geant's roorn.and will also afford a means of reach- ing the rear yard and single constable's quarters in a direct line from police station offices under elver, and without the traversing of any corridors. The arrangement of the cells is such as to permit of their number being added to without materially effecting the structure or the convenience of ar- rangement. In order to allow for the increase of dormitory accommodation for the single constables a space will be left between their quarters and the semi-leteched residences of inspector and sergeant. For the exterior, the materials suggested for adop- tion are brick, externally faced to the front with a good deep red pressed brick with buff terra-cotta dressings. The internal arrangemeuts will be of the plainest and strongest descriptions, and special items, such as cells and their fittings will receive particuler attention. There seems little doubt that under the supervision of Air. IL Burns Dick, the buildings will be erected to the satisfaction of the authorities, and enable the police and other access- ory work to be carried on with efficiency and com- fort. - -

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, DECEMB

In opening the inquiry the Commissioner explained that it was being held not for any nrcial reason con- nected with that parish, but simply in the course of a general inquiry into all the charities of the administra- tive county of Lancashire. The inquiry was instituted in the spring of the present year, and it was being held : at the suggestion and at the request of the County Coun- cil who were c ,Wributing to the expense under the Charity Inquiries Expenses Act, 1892. The present was the third of such inquiries—Systematic county inquiries —which had been held in the country. There was the inquiry into the charities of the West Riding cf York- shire ; the inquiry isto the county of Londe n charities which was still going on; and now there was an inquiry into the charities of the county of Lancaster. Most of the Welsh counties had had these inquiries. The object sought was to put upon permanent record information of the most authentic and complete character as to what charities there were in the county,the property they had and how administered. The result would practically be a new edition brought up to date of the reports of the Charity CommisAioners who many years ago went into every county of England aad Wales, holding public in- quiries. The reports would be issued separately for each parish, and would be issued as Parliamentary papers. THE WARRINGTON GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. " VViggin "at " Warriton " wed to be one of the titbits of the football season in this district, but the Match is not now regarded with quite the sums interest. The Wigan Club has fallen upon evil times and is not the strong organisation it onc as.while even Warring- ton has lost some of its prestige as a club. Saturday's match proved, however, that Warrington can still put a team in the field good enot.ol to gat the better of such stubborn fighters as Wigan. With a little more weight forw.ird, and a couple of really cqoable centre-three• quarters Warrington would be strong enough to win half as many morn matches as they do now. "Wiggin " got their " wiggin " by four tries to nil, aril the game proved interesting. The " gate " wee a little balow par, but there was a goodly crowd all the same.

Warrington had a great advantage in the first half,the spin of the coin giving Bite choice of positions. As a strong breeze blew down the field the home captain did net hesitate to enlist it in his service. The opening ex- changes gave indicati m of a decided superiority on the part of Warrington, and the Wiganerg were rapidly driven back. Bate, who seems to have come to the con- clusion that he might as e'en score tries when he has a chance, doubled round the " scrum " and crossed the line before five minutes had elapsed. This early success pat the home players and spectators on excellent terms with themselves,while the visitors were correspondingly downcast. The wind severely handicapped the Wigan backs, whose kicks were rendered quite abortive. There was some smart work amongst the home backs on several occasions, and they succeeded admirably in pre- venting any combination among their opponents. Leglid, centre, received injuries which aecessitated him leaving the Wigan ranks, and the horaesters theta had things all their own way. Bate again found a weak spot in the defence and slippinw through he passea to Holcroft, the latter having no difß oulty in adding try nun:o9er 2. Be- fore the interval, Harris was given several attempts to show what he was made,of and after one or two fruitless efforts he took the ball from Isherwood and dashed in at the corner. The score at half time was 3 tries to nil in favour of Warrington.

With the c ange of ends it was genor ally expected that Warrington would have their hands full in repelling the Wigan invasion, hot riot from the kick-off the home- eters rushed the ball into the Wigan half. For a quarter of all hour they did all the attaoking and Fisn got a try,as the result of a long transfer from his Captain. Aided by the wind the visir.ore did succeed in getting dosra to the home lino once or twice, but they were proreptly repulsed. There was a lot of punting, but the May Was far from being monotonous. Though both Fishman' Holcroft retired with minor injuries before the closet Warrington continued to hold the whip hand of theirplMonents, an I maintained their twelve points marrn to the end.

" CAPITAL " CHAT.

There is a kind of pcem sung in the London press over the result of Sir Joseph Savory's libel , The verdict or the jury is a vindication of the right of the press to fair and free criticism, and it is a , condemnation of the action of a public man in a , high official position, such as that of Lord Mayer, when he enters upon a course of tratisacti^ns such as formed the subject of inquiry be:ore the court. The ex-Lord Mayor has proved that he was not guilty of corruption during his year of office. lie was not charged with corruption; but his offence against public life and against the dignity ot the office he held was nevertheless very great—so great that the jury emphasised their disapproval of it by not leaving their box.

The proposal by the "Liberal Forwards" to form a club open to both sexes has now been put into practical shape. Premises have been provisionally taken, and adherents of the "forward" movement are being invited to send in applications for mem- bership. The membership will be limited to 500. The promoters of this new organisation seek to avert the growing danger in foreign policy of moral obligation being made subsidiary t 3 material inter- ests, and by means of lectures and discussions they will also endeavour to focus public attention on the social questions of the day.

I have just learned that the originators of the idea of raising a fund of a million guineas for the Purposes of the Wesleyan Methodists have already received the most convincing assurances of success. The fund has not yet been opened, and does not open till January, but £200,000 has been already guaranteed by four districts alone. There are still 28 districts to announce their contributions, and it would not be a matter of very great surprit.e if the susa originally contemplated were exceeded. The sum of .C 200,000 referred to is a minimum guaran- tee, and it is fair to assume that the to: at of the four district s will greatly exceed that amount. The response, so far as it has gone, is quite marvellous, and furnishes a striking object lesson to the wealthy National Church.

Something much mare creative of consternation has occurred among the whole body of civil ser- vants than the addition ot au hour to the daily labour of a number of clerks. It is the compulsory retirement, under the new Order in Council, at a given age, of every per:on of whatever status Lem the service of the State. At least that appears to be the meaning of the Order.. This strikesat 02.1 tbe humbler ranks of serVents, Including even messengers and postmen. The rule of rettrenient has a certain definite and recognised vale's in the upper grades of the service, as it as-its the flow of promotion, although it betters a very small number only of officials compared with the army „Pot men who simply remain where they were. What is to be gained by turning out the members of the humblest ranks of the service, upon whom, it may be, very little physical or mental call is made by their duties, it is difficult to see. But every year now quite a host of men will be cast adrift, and the pension list will come to be the measure by which the new rule will be annually tested.

The new and unexpected difficulties which have arisen in connection with the opening of the grave in the Druce case are likely to throw the problem as far back as ever. All that has hitherto been done may be 80 many necessary steps towards the goal, but the new obstacles appear to render all that has been done very futile. First, there is the Home Secretary's intervention in the matter; nest, a doubt has been raised as to the powers of the di- rectors of the cemetery to allow the grave to be opened ; thirdly, it is said that the cons,mt of the - owner of the vault is necessary ; and fourthly, it is averred that nothing short of an Act of Parliament will allow of the grave being disturbed if the owner refuses to give his consent. Thus the course of jastiCe is barred in all directions, and the condition of our law is reduced to an absulute farce.

THE EXAMINER,

DECEMBER 16th, 1895

THE interpretations put upon Sir William liar- court's Resignation. the letters which have passed between Sir WILLIAM HAR- COURT and Mr. Joirix MonLzy are almost as puzz- ling as the letters themselves. But of the reality of the crisis there cannot be a doubt. Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT has been beset with factions ever since Mr. GLADSTONE'S retirement. In the natural order or things he was his legitimate successor. But what happened we all know. Lord ROSEBERY was " run " for the post of Liberal leader. There is no doubt that the word we have used correctly repre- sents the facts. There was a dinner, a certain newspaper editor was invited, and a campaign was begun which ended in the enthronement of Lord ROSEBERY as the Liberal leader. At the time some of us saw the seeds of mischief in such an arrange- ment, and they have been maturing ever since. Lord ROSEBERY wearied of his curious posi- tion, detached as he was from the active spirits of his party in the Commons. As soon as the defeat of 1895 was seen to be overwhelming, certain sec- tions of the Liberal party in the Commons did not take kindly to Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT's leadership. Some of his colleagues On the same bench voted against him. Of late there have been two sections by no means friendly to his leader- ship—the High Church Liberals and the Scotch Liberals. There have also been Liberals who Wanted a more active Opposition, more frequent challenges to the Government, and more light and leading upon the questions which were perplexing even themselves. Lord Roszimay's recent speeches have revived his popularity, and made some peo- ple say that he was the only possible leader of the Liberal party. Out of this discontent has arisen the mood which has compelled Sir WILLIAM lIAR- CCTRT to say that he is not "a candidate for any Contested position." That is, he does not propose tO allow himself to be " run" against Lord Ross-

EERY or anybody else. He is not disposed to con- tinue a leadership which is to be disputed, be- cause of conflicting sections and interests. That is, if he is to lead the Commons, he must be more cheerfully supported than he has been in the past. Mr. MORLEY agrees with him in his views, and praises him for his skill and patience as "the working leader of the Liberal party. The crisis is one of considerably gravity. It has been gradually coming on, and, as we un- derstand it, Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT is weary of it. If he is to lead in the Commons, and to lead in the country, there must be an end to "personal proscriptions" and divided counsels. Of course, if he be the exciting cause there will be no advan- tage in his continuing to trouble himself with the torments of an unhappy position. Wny should he do so? Mr. GLADSTONE had a similar cabal to face when he reached the critical part of the Home Rule question, and some of the younger Liberals left him to become Unionists. But he was still strong enough to,maintain his position, and the rank and file were loyal and disciplined. The situation is less satisfactory now. Discourage- ' ments and divisions are common, and new ideas I are making their influence felt in many quarters.

CONCLUSION OF AN IN PERESTING CASE. VEitDIC I FOR THE PLAINTIFF. On Tuesday last, his Honour Judge Weenie Fioulkes, resumed the hearing of the case, which ha I cause' so much interest in the distrier, in which it will be remem- bered that George Acton,farmer, Pool Farm, Heath-road Runcorn, brought an action for the recovery of £ll 93 61 from Rober: Shepherd, farmer, Park-side Farm, Aston, the amount of &envier e alleged to be due in rye- pent of damage done to the plaintiff ter ouerh a collision which took place between his trap and a milk fl oat be- longing to the defendant ; it being allege i teat the colli- sion was brought about through the negligent driving of the defendant's servant. CoUnsel having addressed the jury at considerable 1 leneth. His Honour in Summing up said that the action cams after a long time, and it was rather lamentable to think of the amount of money that was being spent in the teeing ef it as compared with the amount of the claim. Of course, there was a lot of conflicting evidenee, and it remained for them (tee jary) to deeide which side hail been nommitting perjury. It wag a question for them ro decide whether the defendant had been guilty of negligence or net. Having reviewed the whole of the evidence his Honour charged the jury to bring in their verdict. After a brief retirement the jury returned into court, and the foreman Mr. M. L. Davies stated that they were agreed upon their verdict which was for the plain- tiff. His Honour accordingly entere jnelement for the plaintiff for the full amount of the The Court rose at about quarter to five, after sitting the whole of the day with a single adjournment of half- an-honr. H s Honour having hiehly complimented tee jury, up, n their petience and diligence, discharged them.

===== 0002601_18981216_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
A LIBERAL leader must be in ; Tlf, Liberal tho House of Commons. There is Leader. no escaping from this position. The battle will be in the popular House, and every point in the game must be watched there with a steadfast eye. The House of Lords only meets for a short time every working day, and the political situation is not to be studied there. A peer can- not always be in attendance to receive reports of what is going on in the Commons, and to counsel as to the tactics to be adapted. It would be an intolerable position for any man to have to con- duct operations in this fashion. Nor can a peer ever command that popular confidence which is essential to the unity and the enthusiasm of th Liberal party. The plan has been tried, and it ha not answered. It would be a great mistake to try it again. There are, however, some persons who want to repeat the blunder, and who think that a few brilliant speeches have so far changed tne situation as to make a second experiment likely to be more hopeful than the first. We hope they will hesitate before they finally commit them- selve, to this course. The peer will always feel a if he were outside the party he is professedly lead- ihg. This was the chief characteristic of Lord ROSEBERY. He advised others, he sketched a policy for them, but identification, heart and soul, with the policy lie advocated always seemed to be lacking. He left the Liberals to follow his advice, and went back to his other pursuits. It might be different another time, but we doubt it. A real leader must make of his politics something better than an episode in his life. What we want is a commoner, entirely devoted to the cause he takes up. We shall suggest no names. It is only with the utmost reluctance that we have used any name. But as to the principle involved we have not the faintest shadow of a doubt. Be he young or old, the new Liberal leader, and the true leader must be a member of the House of Commons, in constant tcuch with his party there, and ready to take his part where the fighting is to be done. To sit in the Lords, with a party of about thirty Liberals left, is enough to dispirit any man. To be in the Commons, where there is always some- thing stirring, and a member of the body which makes and unmakes Ministries, is to be at the centre of political activity. When a situation is so perplexing as the Liberal situation is just now, it is as well to grasp one clear, saving principle. There is no" personal proscription" in it. If Mr. GLADSTONE had ever accepted a peerage in middle life his later career would have been different. Lord SALISBURY may seem a contradiction, but he has to be content with forming one of a triumvi- rate which leads the Unionist party, and the Liberal cry is for one leader and not a triumvirate. Besides, there is a permanent Conservative majority in the Lords.

Mr. Ascroft has been humorous about the amount of devotion which is necessary from a member of Parliament. He complains, among other things, that a member is expected at the same time to be in the House of Commons and never away from his constituency. He is required to support all the movements in his district, to answer correspondence on every conceivable sub- ject, to be able to introduce deputations to Minis- ters, and, above all things, to have a large purse ; or else, Mr. Ascroft might have added, to make it known that he has a very small one for the Labour members get along very well. But, after all, this is an extreme view of the case. Only a small pro- pertion of the members returned to the House can speak with any ability, and it is well for the pro- gress of business that so few can. It is quite possible to explain this seeming deficiency by gett- ing appointed on some private committee, which may be supposed by the public to exhaust your time. It is also possible to give oneself an indi- viduality by asking a number of questions ; the public, curiously enough, does not take this for a display of ignorance, but of learning. It is also possible, if one is a county landlord, to distribute game more effectively than words of rhetoric. Then with a good election agent, personal grasp of poli- tics becomes a subsidiary matter.

The members of the Warrington Board of Guar- dians—or at least the majority of them—are par- ticularly anxious that their names should be handed down to posterity. That is the impression created by their decision to erect tablets bearing their illustrious names in the entrance hall of the new hospital. One would have thought that the inscriptions on the two foundation stones which have been graven in letters which will not be effaced for generations to come,would havebeen sufficient. Belt the Board has thought otherwise, and all who enter the portals of the new infirmary will be re- minded of the great and wise men who composed the Board of Guardians in the year of our Lord 1898, and who spent £30,000 of the ratepayers' money in erecting that institution. One member did piotest against this kind of immsrtality but the protest fell upon deaf ears and was of no avail. The order has gone forth for the tablets!

There are some people who, their sympathies in a cause once enlisted, will do more on the im- pulse of a moment than they would for almost any other consideration. Proof of this :theory has been furnished lately. Last Wednesday week, Mr. Brighouse voluntarily appeared at Warrington County Petty Sessions to defend a man charged with neglecting his children, and whose wife is at present undergoing a sentence of three months imprisonment on the same charge. As a Coroner Mr. Brighouse is respected—in the widest sense of thc word—and as an advocate he has repeatedly played himself most able. The defendant was a Burtonwood man, and at the time the Inspector visited the house he saw the dead body of a chi Id. Mr. Brighouse explained that the reason of his appearance in the case was because, from the press reports of the case, the public might think he had neglected his duty as a Coroner in not holding an inquest on the child. His shrewd and clever cross-examination of the witnesses made the fact more apparent that an inquest had not been necessary and ultimately secured the dismissal of the defendant. We may say Dr. Valentine. also volun- tarily attended, and was prepared to give evidence if required.

TH I lAA MINER FRI DA lir,

It cannot be said that the members of the War- rington Board of Guardians have emerged credit- ably from their strictures upon the Ladies Com- mittee. In the first place the statemcnt that there has been friction between the raembers of the Ladies' Committee and the oiThials of the Union seems to have been a myth; whilst the refusal to sanction the appointment of the three ladies sub- mitted for their approval to be added to the Ladies Committee can only be characterised as puerile. It will have the result of retarding the useful work the ladies have taken in hand, which, instead of being opposed should at all times receive the cordial support of the Guardians. Since the for- mation of the Ladies Committee the attitude of eertain Guardians towards the committee's work has been anything but sympathetic,and the sooner a kindlier disposition is shown the better it will be for the proper administration of the Poor LAW'S.

The victory of Runcorn over Swinton was not half a one.. True, Rune )rn did not get a try —and of late tries have been strangers to the Runcorn three-quarters—yet the victory was well deserved and Runcorn were clearly that many points super- ior to tAair adversaries, and should be accorded warm praise.

Per some time the turning point has been looked forwar I to. Every recurring S iturlay those ar- dent ones to whom the club is user and dear have exoec•.ed to see that form which gladdened their hearts on Saturday last. There was an exhi- bition of the truism that" Runcorn breeds foot- ballers;" and although there are' Runcorn players of fame and renown wh3 stand aloof, and others who have souzht fields and pastures new., it is pleasiag to note and thank that there still rem iins amongst us that metal which ma le the Runcorn team of old. once the dread of the Rugby Union. -CORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

SPECIAL. MEETING.

A 'eclat meeting of the Runcorn • Urban District coil was held on Thursday morning at the Town Rauconvened for the purpose of affixing the seal of the Coul to a. TYPOGRAPHICAL SOCIETY.

At the Blue Bell lintel, Nevvtun,on Saturday, the annual dinner in connection with the Newton-le- Willows branch of the Typographical Society was held, about 70 persons patting in an attendance. After the meal Mr B Lowe presided and was sup- ported by Mr. W. Tasker (Branch secretary) and others. The loyal toasts having been proposed and duly honoured, Mr. Lidderad proposed "The Typo- graphical association" and mentioned that in the call for reforms it practically led the way. The toast was responded to by Mr. Rose (secretary of the Wigan Branch) in the absence of the representa- tive from the Executive Council. Mr. Frampton gave the toast of the Newton branch and referred to its rapid growth. Mr. German re•ponded. The toast of "The visitors" was proposed by Sir. F. Eon and responded to by Mr. E. Grace. The rest of the evening was spent in a convivial manner. FATALITY AT HAYDOCK.

Mr. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at the Wagon and florss Hotel, Haydock, on Monday, on , the body of Robert Wilon, collier, who lived at I 335 Crow-lane, Eirlestown, and who was killed at the Wood Pit, Haydock, belonging to Messrs. R. Evans and Co, on Friday morning.—Matthaw Moran, of 306, Or )w-lane, Earlestown, said that the accident happened at about 2 o'clock on Friday morning in the drawing road. Witness and de- ceased worked together and witness was out get- ting some prop an I when he returned a fall had occurred and deceased was under it, The fireman had been through at about half past eight. When the fireman visited the place, there was a little dirt dawn,and he instructed witness to set a prop,which he did. Witness had wclh.ked in that part of the mine since Monday ni;ht.—John Shaw, fireman, , said he had inspected the place at nine o'clock on Thursday night. He then found that a small fall had bccurre 1 at the spot where deceased was after- wards killed. Witness told the man who was work- ing there to clear the dirt away and 4, put a prop up and to make the roof hacu7e.—A verdict of" Ac- cidental death "was returned.

THE LATCHFORD ROBBERIES.-SENTENCE ON

BA.RBEIL Torn Barber (26), labourer, was indicted at Liver- pool Assizes on Wednesday, for having, at Warring- ton, between the 16:h and 17th November, broken into, and entered the booking office of the Latch- ford Station of the Lmdon and North-Western Railway C•Jrnpiny, and stolen a suit 61 clothes and a ja,..ket • al,o with having stolen a bay mare at Tnelwalf, and two geese at Granenhall. Prisoner pleaded guilty, it was stated that Barber who lived in the outskirts of Warrington, was quite a pest in the neighbourhood. He did very little work, but practically lived on the proceeds of small rob- beries. At the time of his arrest he was wearing clothes stolen from a canal boat.—The Judge com- mitted him to gaol for twelve months.

A MONTH FOR WATCH-SNATCHING.

At the W-arr-i7g-to—in -13-o;oUg-h---Policr-e-diTin-rt- on Monday before Mr. F. Monks (in the caair) and other magis- trates, William Kenny, Ship-yard, was charged with having stolen from the person of Georale Davison on Sunday night a silver watch and chain, valued at £2 —Prosecutor said he was a moulder living at 28, Vic- toria-street. About t m o'clock on Sunday night he went up Ship-yard and into Bank-street, with the intention of going to his cousin's house. He fell down in the yard being under the influence of drink. The prisoner Came up and took the watch out of his waistcoat pocket. The chain was att ached to the watch. Prisoner said " I will take it to your cousin." Prisoner ran off, and ,he followed him. He saw him go into a lodging- house. Prisoner was not fully dressed. He (prosecutor) afterwards picked him out in the lcdging-house and gave him into custody. Thomas Mtcheil,brick ayer,lB,Lower Ship-yard, said he heard Davison call out, and saw a man,who had ooly his trousers on, running in the direction of the lodging. house. Dsvison came up and said that the man had stolen his watch and chain. They went to the lodging- house and Dsvison picked out 'Amer.—John Evans, who was with the last witness, gave corroborative evi- dence.—lrisnector Taylor said be received a complaint from Devisom,and proceeded to the lodging house, where he found the prisoner in bed. Before he spoke prisoner Bald " I have not got his watch nor clock either." Wit- ness searched the house,but did not find the watch. He took prisoner to the Police S-ation. He then returned and made a search of the neighhourhood, eventoally finding the watch and Chain in an entry. He afterwards charged the prisoner with having stolen the watCh an chain, and he replied " I have nothing to say. I was ,nrver in his company in my ifs." He had previously , said he saw Devison drunk in the enlry.—Prisoner now Faid he had not been out of the house that night.—He ; was committed to gaol f a month.

NRIVTON'-LE• WILLOWS V. ORFORD BARRACKS .-119 above match, in the second round of the Warrington Cap was played at Nursery Park, Newton, on Saturday before a fair gate.
