TERRIBLE OUTRAGE AT ST. HELENS. A GIRL MURDERED.

It was in the district surrounding Park-road, Parr, that great excitement and indignation pre- vailed on Sunday when it gradually leaked out that early in the merning the body of a little girl named Sarah Flaherty, of 6, Black Horse-street, had been fouud lying dead in a field behind Park-road, amid surroundings which indicated that the girl had not only been the victim of a terrible outrage, but bad subsequently been done to death by suffocation. The ghastly discovery was made by a man named Greenall, residing in Lathom-street, and inquiries and investigations fully confirmed the worst fears as to the horrible nature (31' the crime, which had bean perpetrated during the preceding night. It appears that the little girl Flaherty,who would have been seven years of age next April, left her parents' houe on Saturday night to visit the home of her uncle, Mr. Peter Barker, residing in Park- road—a distance of about 150 yards away. The child had often gone thither to play with her cousins, and she reached there all right about six , o'clock. She remained for about three hours,when, on the advice of her brother Tom, a lad of 17 years rof age, she started to walk home. Park-road is one of the main, populous thorough- fares leading into the town from the Haydock and Parr districts, and particularly on Saturday night lis much frequented, and is in no sense a quiet or llonely-street. But, after leaving her uncle's house, 1 the little girl does not seem to have been again seen alive, except by her cruel murderer, and all , trace of her subsequent movements is for the pre- sent shrouded in mystery. As the girl did not arrive home the parents made inquiries from several neighbours, but as she had several times stayed overnight at her uncle's and her grandparents' houses very little anxiety was felt as to her safety. It was about eight o'clock on Sunday morning that Thomas Greenall, a collier, of Lathom-street, was : sauntering through the field when he saw the body ,of a child lying in a corner, at the farthest point from the backs of the houses in Park-road. Seeing that the child was dead and cold he at once gave an alarm, and it was early ascertained that the little girl was Sarah Flaherty. The police and par- ents were informed of the discovery, and the'father on visiting the spot, was horrified to find that the rumour which had reached him that it was his daughter was only too well founded. The father carried the body home, but shortly afterwards the Ipolice authorities had it remoied to the mortuary at the Town Hall pending a post-mortem examina- tion and the coroner's inquiry into the circum- stances surrounding the case. The field is situated behind the houses in Park- road, and was formerly used as a football ground by the old Park-road Association Club. It is cur- rounded by a chemical waste-heap " wall," broken down and dilapidated in many places, and was practically open to any one and everyone. In the meantime Chief Constable Wood and the officers under him instituted the closest inquiries and search, but up to a late hour on Sunday night no arrest in connection with the terrible affair had been effected. There is plenty of grass growing at the soot where the body was found, and there are no traces of any struggle. It is not known whether the crime was committed there, or whether the little victim met her fate elsewhere, and that her lifeless body was placed where it was found in order to add to the mystery surrounding her death. On Sunday afternoon an outline of the facts was communicated to the county coroner, who imme- diately ordered a post-mortem examination of the body, and this was made by Drs. Reid and Masson later in the day. Though the authorities decline to state the result of the examination,it is stated on reliable authority that the little girl was shock- ingly outraged, and that her death was due to suffocation.

THE INQUEST.-NO TRICE OF THE

MURDERER.

The inqucst was opened at St. Helens Town Hall on Tuesday before M. Samuel Brighouse,(County Coroner). The jury lint met at the Town Hall, and afterwards went, in company wirh the coroner, to the . mortuary at the rear of the building to view the body. The greatest interest centred in the proceedings, and, although ths morning was raw and foggy, a large num- ber of people assembled in trout of the Town The procee lings were delayed an hour leer than the time originally fixed. Mr. C. Ralshaw was tne foreman of the jury, no fewer than fifteen tradesmen having been empanelled. The witnesses an Dr. Reid were all prwent in good time. [ho jury having viewed the body, The C -ironer read the poUce report of th case which briefly entitled the story of the crime. Hs tin 1.1 said that he understool from Inspector Steele, who had charge of tho case, that on to the present they WPT3 not able to bring before the jury any person whom they could charge with having caused the death of the deceased, and he felt c rain they would understand that it was absolutely non ,es try that every care should be taken if possible to find the person who had caused the death of the decea 1, becauFe titer, was no doubt ab sot it that somebody had caused it. After taking the evi- dence of the facts and tho evidence of Dr. Reid, who had made a post-mortem he proposed to adjourn the inquiry. After some discussion he slirl he would adjourn the in- quiry until Thursday, the 29th inst., at 9.15 a.m. Ellen Flaherty, mother of the deceased, said hyr child was six years of age last April. On Saturday night wit- ness went shopping. On getting back to the house at 9-40 she found the child was missing, and searchrd for her until midnight. On Sunday morning she went searching for the girl again, and when she was coming back again she RAW the people bringing the child home, and fell down earning. She had no suspicion of anyone in connection with the death. Martin Flaherty, the gin's father, raid that on Satur- day night he was somewhat under the influence of drink. ' He went home and found his wife searching th I street about midnight, and took her into the house. They did not go out again till booming, when he was called and found the girl's body in the field. He had no sus- nicion as to who had committed the outrage. He sent for Constable Jackson and Dr. Gilloran. Thomas Flattert., brother, spoke to having sent his

THE MEDICAL EVIDENCE.—THE MURDERER'S

FINGER MARKS. Dr. James Reid said hesaw the child's body on Sunday naorning a: 11.30 at Black Horse-street. The face wag livid, particularly the lips, and tiere was dried blood about the lower parts of the body, and a patch of stain on the under garment. In the afternoon, along with Dr. Masson, he made a post-mot tern at the mortuary to which ho had rsqussted that the body should be removed. The body was fairly wal nourished, rigor mortis being well marked, and there were deep post* mortem stains on the back. On the left forearm on the 'outer Eide below the elbow there was a bruised patch about one inch in diameter. Immediately below this there was a recent crescentic excoriation about 3-16th of an inch in length. A little below that was a similar excoriation. There were some irregular shaped bruiser on the front and right side of the neck, and a large quantity of blood frothy and mucous flowt d from the nostrils. The doctor west on to detail the examination, which showed that an outrage of the most savage and brutal description bad been committed on the child by some man. The larynx was full of blood and froth, and lungs, brain, and all toe internal organs were deeply g este d. the StAin on the undergarment was bbod. etThohene cause of death was asphyxia. The Coroner : Bow do you think asphyxia wg, brought about? Do yo a think by violence applied tO the throat ? Dr. Reid : I think more likelx bs suffocation. There ar3 marks on the throat sh wring toat it had been forced back, but from thg internal signs and the condition of the larynx and so on, 1 believe she had been suffocated by:the mouth. The Coroner ; Are you able to say bow long the child had been deed?

_ Witness : S .tnewlizre about twebro or eighteen hours._

VISIT OF 111 R. HENRY BROAD- HURST, MP., 'CO WAR- RINGTON. ENTHUSIASTIC LIBERAL MEETING IN PARR HALL. At the Parr Hall, Warringtm, Mr. Henry Broadhurst M.P., was the principal ape ater at a meeting held wider the auspices of the Warrington. Liberal Aesoziatien. There WAS a crowded attendance, and much enthusiasm display( d. The proceedings were presided over by Mr. John Crosfield, J.P., and he was supported on the plat- form by a large number of ladles and gentlemen includ- ing Rev J Yoga, E J Gia st, J. P., Hey. W H Drum- mond, Thomas Birchall, J.P., Robert Garnett, J.P., A H Cresfield,CapSain Redger, alderman B Roberts,J.P. Councillors Plumpton, James Evans, J Shaw Green, J Wilkinson, A Petcock, F R Roberts. D S Duncan, D Tin- nion, L Edison, Messrs. Jackson Holmes, Mountfield, Joseph Owen, Dr. Gonna, J.P., Stranee, C E Parker, William Furness, T B Malkin, Dale, A Corney, A Rsthbon, F Dixon, F Brown, Thomas DAs, William Jeffery, G Albinson, H Hill, J W Bouchier, T H Granger, W Isaac, C Healey, E Plinstoa, J W Jeffery, M Butterfield, H Rathbone, Bate, J Longland. C Loegehaw, A Downs, R Travis, T Hawthorne, Crosskill,W E Pliastm, T Smith, T Dale, and John Clayton. Letters of apology had ben received from Mr. Ziba Armitage J.P., the Rev J S Matthews, and Councillors W Pierpoint. F W Monks Messrs. J Platt J P, C J M orris JP,Mr J Car Liston, Bleier 7 Thornton, Lient. George Crosfield, Rev William Potts, Fether H B Polding, Rev J T Schofield, T B Carter, J C Young, A H Lee, Peter Caldwell, Joseph Hughes wed others. The CHAIRMAN said he had a pleasing duty tre perform; that was to introduce to them his fr.end Mr. Br cad. burst, M.P. (Cheers.) He was what was called a work- ingmen member of Parliament —(cheers)—ind was a very fine representative of the workingmen. (Cheers.) He was respected in the House of Commode—and justly respected—because he possessed the cornmonsence of not attempting to speak upon any subject in the House when he was not thoroughly acquainted with it. (Laughter. He (the chairman) had not much acquaintance with the House of Commons, and he had no desire to have any acquaintance with it. (Laughter.) But be believed the House disliked extremely a man who was constantly getting on his feet end attempting to talk about a sub- ject which he didn't understand. They had a little of that to complain of somethnes in their own local Perna- ment—(laughter) —and he often wished that the speeches they had to listen to there were condensed to abut one third or one quarter of their length. (Renewed laugh- ter) He now wished to call their attention for a few minutes to a speech delivered on the 18:h N member in Manchester by the Right El mane:able-3e cane 1 —Joseph Chamberlain. (Hisses). In his opinion tee proper title for that gentleman would be Dis-Honoerable and not Henour- able. (Laughter). Mr. Ch imberlain had the audacity and impudence to say th-at it was Mr. Disraeli who laid the seals of the docttine in " Sybil," and distinctly put before the Conservative party as the object of their future policy, that while .ainting aside those revolution- ' ary or censtitutional changes they should make them- selves the meurhpiece of the-popular feeling in favour of social and 4smstc reform. Although Mr. Disraeli found the party under his guidance slow to educate, yet undoubtedly they made progress under his guidance and under the subsequent guidance of men like Lord Ran- dolph Churehill they had now arrived at a position in which they :might fairly claim that it was to their legislation that the greatest of the social reforms now expressed in the Statute book of this country were due. (Laughter.) He (the chairman) thought that was a stagger( r when he read it. (Laughter.) He had lived long enough to know what the Liberal party had done and what the Tory party had done. Even before his time if they turned to the year 1832 ths great reform of Lord John Russell was certainly a Liberal measure. What happeced then in the House of Lords ? The whole of the Tory benches were empty; there wasn't a single one present he hear the till read. And that was one of the greatest social reforms that was ever carried in this country, (Cheers.) Since then they had seen the stamp duty taken off newspapers, which had enabled the working class to get a penny paper containing all the news of the weed. The Tory party resisted it as much as they could. They didn't want the English working- man to be educated. Then that great leader of theirs —Mr. Gladetone—(cheers)—freed from duty over twelve hundred articles; that were imported into this country ; duties that had then imposed by the Tory landlords when they had the whole power of the country in their hands. The landlords cared for nobody but. themselves.: (Laughter.) They wanted to protect their own pockets and the people were to pay. (Hear, hear.) In his opinion every great measure of social reform had been passel by the Liberal party with tee exception of Disraeli's bill in 1867 when he reduced the franchise to household suffrage, on purpose, as he called it" to dish the Whigs." (Laughter). That was really the only good social reform that he could remember as being passed by the Tories. And yet, Mr. Chamberlain had the audacity to say that it was the Conservative party that they had to thank for their social reforms. He wondered Mr. Chamberlain didn't choke. (Laughter.) What had that great statesman done? He had given them a Compel:nation Bill—one of the most iniquitous things that was ever passed through the House ot Com- mons. No Liberal Government would have passed that. Mr. Chamberlain eared nothing for the employers of labour ; but he took good care to stop before he got to the agricultural labourers. (Laughter.) He was afraid of offending the landlords. He cared nothing about toe manufacturers of Lancashire and Yorkshire. He was playing for his own hand and thought he would catch the votes of the working-classes by bringing in the Com- pensation Bill—("tot ")—but he was deceived. And there were plenty of Tories who did not like i hat measures. One of them was Lord Londonderry who condemned him in (the strongest language for br.ne'ing in such a measure as that. In 1835, when Mr. Chamberlaia was a member of Mr. Gladstone's government he started a campaign in the country with an unauthorised programme which he had never shown to Mr. Gladstone. Mr. Chamberlain made three great speeches on it r one at Birmingham. one here at War- rington—Some of them remembered him, he dareseet— and one at Inverness. When Mr. Chamberlain came to Warrington they asked him to avoid two particular questions which they did not want talking about. Mr. Chamberlain selected those two questions and none other when he made his speech. (Laughter.) He simply smashed them in Warrington with his speech. (Laugh- ter.) Ile (the Chairman) had wished him many a time since at Jericho. (Laughter.) For the last 15 years Mr. Chamberlain had been thirsting to be the Prime Minister. That was his object when he started the cam- paign in the country in 1835. He thought he could trip Mr. Gladstone up but lee found the "grand old man" was too strong for him. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. Chamberlain 101 l upon his own back and Could stop there 'for the rest of his life so far as he (the Chairman) was concerned. The Tory party did not trust him to-day. They would not have him ; and he thought he was speaking the voice of Lancashire Liberals when he said that they would not have him. (applaueen They would never take Joseph Chamber- lain back into their ranks they might depend upon it. Thirteen years ago he (the chairman) said publicly that the " Times" newspaper wa3 the biggest liar in England (Laughter.) He was told he would catch it for that ; there would be an action for libel, he was told. He re plied :hat the " Times" could bring an action to- morrow. When Mr. Gladstone introduced his Horns Rule measure the "Times" callcd them separatists. It was a lie. (Hear, hear.) They never were separatists and they were not to-day. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Cham- berlain called himself a Liberal Unionist. He was cer- tainly not a Liberal; he hadn't an atom of Liberalism in him,and was no more a Unionist than he (the chairman) was. He was neither a traitor nor a turncoat. (Cheers.) If Mr. Gladstone had only called that Home Rule bill a national County Council bill it would have been carried and would have been existing to-lay. Whet had the Tory party done just now? They had introduced a keel government measure in Ireland. It was a stepping stone to a central council in Dublin. (Hear, hear.) Whet was wanted was that the Irish people should be able to pass Bills for making rail- ways, canals, harbours, or things of that sort which were necesmry for the country without being obliged to take their witnesses to London in order to pass them through the House of Commons. (Hear, hear.) The object was to relieve the English House of Commons of that work and let the Irish people do it for themselves. (Cheers.) As for the idea of separation the Liberals never dreamed of it. They never wished it or wanted it and therefore it was a lie to say they were separatists. (Cheers.) The people of this country would find it out some day. Many thought tree Liberal party was all in pieces. They might chuckle, but there was bone and sinew and power in the Liberal party that they didn't dream of. (Hear, hear.) He held that the Liberal • party in England was the backbone of the country. (Hear, hear.) They were perfectly loyal subjects ; they only desired the welfare and prosperity of the people. (Cheers.) It was exactly the very opposite that the Tory party sought for. The Terkel wished to protect their own pockets ; they didn't ears for the people of this country, and Mr. Chamberlain would not be ac- cepted by the Tories, and would never be Prime Minis- ter of England as long as be lived. (Applause.) The REV. JOHN YONGE then moved the following reso- lution e " This meeting offers a cordial welcome to

Councillor CROSFIELD : D 3 you mean teohnical by that?

Mr. MONKS, in reply, sal I that what was wanted was a schoel in which a bay who had passed the 7th standard could still carry on hie elucetion under conditions mere fav our ble, for the development of his mind, and be taught subjects which were quite out of the reach of purely elementary schnols. He would not confine this to boys. (iFer, heir.) He knew th ,re was a great wait in Warrington for higher education for the girls. (Ap- plause). If they had such a school as he referred to, taueht by men of University standing, they could bring their pupils to the Museum and there give them such an object lesson as would shorten the time of their stu ly enormously. This Higher Grade School would be lin a sense, a Kindergarten for youths of 14 to 117, and in that way he was satisfied they could make great progress in the education of Warring- ton. Anyone who had visited Switzerland, Germany, America, or Canada, and seen what was going on there must come back with the feeling that so far as we were concerned in Warringtoa we were being hopelessly left behind. That was not the condition of things they wished to Fes in the town, and it was not a condition of thiues they would like to see throughout the country. Ile hoped that through the instrumentality of the Isr- paratien and by the aid of a special rate, and the grant from the County Council, funds would be provided for the establishment of a Higher Grade Scholl. He had came to the conclusion that a 2d. rate, with the grant from the Council, would enable them to do it. They would have to provide a suit able building in which to carry on the work, and he hoped this was not beyond the means of Warrington. He could only speak for him- self, but wh n tee time came he would be p-epared to take his full share of the responsibility. (Loud ap- plause.) Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD proposed the toast of " Success to toe Museum and Library." In &thee so he thought there had net been a time when the advantages of the Library were better realised than at present. We had a cheap press, cheap literature, and he really thought we were suffering from a surfeit of what he must call the " Tit Bits " class of reading. The people took their reading by snacks, and he teodeht it was a good thing they had an opportunity of getting a solid meal at the public Library. (Hear, hear.) He supposed it was a treisra tog ly that the Library was teeth a means to an end, and an end in itself. A man who was well informed and cultivated had the best chance in the long run of getting on, and besides the means to an end it wee an end itself because, as Huxley had said, " literature is the most enduring tome of refined pleasure." (Applause.) Alluding to a visit which he and his rather had paid recently to Mr. Glazebraok Rylands, Mr. Crosfield said that Mr. Rylancls took as active and keen interest in the Mu ieum and Library as ever he did, though he was far advanced into the hale old age which was a symbol of our English life. It was very interest- ing to them to hear Mr. Ryland s describe hew the Museum grew bit by bit in its initial stage. It struck him (the speaker) that the development of these early beeinaings and the progress in the succees of the Museum and Library was perhaps analae,ous to the ext•ao:dinary development we had seen in every part of the country during the last 50 years. (Applause.) Mr. b. GREENING responded, and expressed the obli- gation of the members of the Committee to Mr. Gros- field for the way in which he had proposed the toast. He said that one of the most curious changes bathe ideas respecting museums during the last 50 years was that they were not now looked upon as mere curiosity shops, or dumping grounds for curiosities. Our Museum, he was glad to say stood in the first rank amongst the technical and scientific institutions of the country. The Museum Committee had now a definite scheme to work upon. Their objects were three Fist, thel formation of a local collection in which they placed specimens of all sorts of animals which exist in the district, also geo- logical specimens showing the geology of Warrington ; also Warrington antiquities, prehistoric and Roman,ani more recent. From this way, they learned something of the progress of the neighbourhood. The second objeat was to form an exhibition collection of types of animals, plants, and antiquities so that a visitor could walk through the rooms and take a bird's-eye view of the mar- vels of ,life,and get a rapid idea of the geological and zoo- logical schemes. The third object was a students' or re- served coll^ction, where students and specialists could study the various forms of life. In connection with this collections the generosity of Warringtoa people was very valuable. The Warrington Museum had been able to seal up to the National Collection many rare and valuable specimens. Ha hoped that in the future local patriotism would influence the men of Warrington to be generous, but he was sure that when they met 50 years hence thy would not entertain warmer intrilings for their Chairman than they did that night. (Applause). Councillee &raw GREEN said he felt greatly honoured at being asked to respond. Histerically, as Mr. Belmont had often told them, Waniaeton held first place among the homes of authors and readers. Prior to 1848 there were three small libraries in the town, one of 300 volumes which were distributed twice a week by Mr. Howard, a private lending library of the same size kept by Mr. Gardner, and the town library which had been spoken of. This was all the provision for readers in Warrington at that time. The speaker then reviewed the growth of the library from the time the committee of the town library made the first move towards founding the present Institution, and mentioned in :eoeclusion his conviction that the opportunity thus afforded would be improved by all clams of the people and the never-to- be forgotten spirit and ,e.lory of the old town would come back, increase,and foster. (Applause). Mr. R. PIERFOINT, M.P.,tOSSLNI "The Mayor and Cor- poration of Warrington," and said that a Mayor in accepting office took upon himself no light task, and a member of the Corpor ttien when he took up his office accepted a very heavy task indeed, and he thought that every member of the community was deeply b3holden to those gentlemen who were willing to toke up the hard work and heavy responsibility entailed by the offices they held. (Hear, hear.) Ha was one of those who believed that almost without exception in our municipal work, gentlemen took upon ihemselwee this offiee entirely f r tho public good, perhaps impressed a little with the personal honour, but without the slightest intention of making a single penny. (Applause.) Allud- ing to the Mayor, Mr. Pierpoint said he had known him for a great meny yearn—the beginning of their intimacy wis lost in tine dim vista of the past. (Laughter.) He had known His Worship always as an honest naan,faith- ful friend,and kind-hearted gentleman—one whose sym- pathies went out to all whether rich or poor. (Applause.) The Meson responded feefingly to the toast, aril allu- ded to the pleasure it gave him to meet Mr. Monks, who he had known since boyhood, and whose examole he had tried co fellow. He always looked upon Mr. Monks as one of Vve noblest sons of Warrington. He,the speaker clainvel to be omen himself and he was proud of the good old town, and proud to be present at the 50th Anniver- sary of the establishment of the Museum. The School of Art he owed a deep debt of gratitude,and he was very glad to See his old friend Mr. Thompson present there that night. (Applause.) Referring to the Council, the Mayer said he believed the work was carried on to-day as successfully as ever it was, and though the members were imbued with one idea and that was te sink all poli- tical leelings and do all ia their power for "the good old town.

Cuncillor CROSFIELD said it was a pleasure to him to be there that night, as this was a red-letter day in the history of Warrington. Tney had celebrated the dia- mond jubilee of the Queen, and now they were celebrat- ing the jubilee of the Museum and Library, art insitu- tine which was a credit to the town and invaluable to the people. His old friend Mr. tilazebrook Rylands told h.m that they were celebrating not the jubilee of the Museum, but the diamond jubilee because he to ok to collecting books for the Library 60 ye ire ago in the old Cloth Hall Yard, off Bridge-street. (Applause.) He there accumulated,also, specimens as a nucleus for the future museum for Warrington. Amongst them was a slab of red sandstone bating the imprint of the foot of an ichthyosaurue—(laughter)—which should be in the Library yet. Mr. Ryland' interest was as keen and strong to-day as it was when he was a young man 20 years of age. He would have been present that night had health cermitted him. They regretted the absence not only of a min like Mr' Ryland's, but of Mr. William Robson, who was now 85 years of age, and who also took a keen interest in everything that affected the educational and intellectual welfare of the people of Warringtom (Applause.) Also they regretted the absence of Mr. Robert Davies. (Hear, hear.) He con- cluded by proposing the" Health of our Guests," rr- marking that the committee were pleased to see them °specialty the member for the Borough. (Applause.) They forgot difference.; about politics, thouela he dared say that Ile and:Mr. Pierpoint could give them a dish for an hour or two. YOUNG respended to the toast in a very humorous speech, remarking at the close that a man must be blini if he did rant see amend him ample evi- dence of good progress. LATEST NEWS.

THE WARK CASE, MISS YATES'S WILL. It is stated that the will of the late Miss Yates will shortly be subject of law proceedings. Probate has not yet been obtained, but that will, no doubt, be done in due course. Subsequently, steps will be taken to US* the validity of the legacy in favour of Lieutenant Wark, The total estate does not amount t) mach over £2.000, land the sum bequeathed to Lieutenant Wark is half of the residue—namely, between £5OO and £6OO. The case Will be held in the Palatine Chancery Court, and the Vice-Chancellor will be asked to grant a declaration that the legacy is nullified by the conviction of Wark for the Wilful murder of Miss Yatte3.—lt is further stated that notice of the pending litigation has been served at We!., ton Gaol upon Lieutenant Wark, IMPORTANT LETTER FROM SIR WILLIAM HA.RCOURT. Tba Editor c f the" South Wales Daily News " has re. ceived the following letter from Sir William Harcourt. Malwood, Lyndhurst, December 14, 1898. " Dear Sir.— You will see by my published letter to Mr. Morley that I have no intention of leaving the House of Cornmons, and that I hope still as an independent member of the House of Commons to deserve the confi- dence of my constituents in West Monmouth. Ido not feel that there would be any advantage for the present in my making any further statement than that which I have already made public.—Signed, W. V. HABCDURT." DEPARTURE OF THE NEW VICEROY. Lord Curzon of Kedleston left London yesterday en route for Bombay. The new Viceroy of India will Join Lady Curzon and family at Marseilies. DEATH OF LORD VERNON. Lord Vernon died at Bournemouth yesterday after- noon. He was the seventh baron, and was born in 1854. In 1892-94 he was captain of her. Majesty's Honourable Corps of Gentleman-at-Arms, and he was also a member of the Royal Commission on Agricultuiral Depression. The country seats of the family are Sud- bury-Hall, Derby and Poynton Hall, Stockport, The deceased baron married a New York lady. His eldest son was born in 1888.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to toe Editor. Matter intended f3r publication must be written on one side only of the paper. Correspmdentr are str mgly ad yds: dto write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one. CHRISTMAS TREE AND ENTERTAINMENT FOR POOR CHILDREN. Sir,—lt has been suggested that it would be desirable to emulate the efforts which are made in Manchester and other large towns to brighten the lives of the poor- est of the poor children in our midst. With this end in view it is proposed to give a Christmas tree and enter- tainment in the Parr Hall, or other suitable place, as soon as can be arranged. To enable this to be done, it is, of course, necessary to raise sufficient funds for the purpose, and those who are charitably disposed and willing to assist in this object may send their contributio .8 to me. Mr. Henry Taylor, P.C.M. ,of 188 Longford-street has promised to act as hon. secretary and distribute the tickets amongst the poor children, irrespective of sect or creed, and I have consented to act as hon treasurer. Would you kindly assist us to bring brightness and happiness amongst those, who are unable to help them- selves ? All donations will be acknowledged trcugh the medium of the press.—Yours etc., W. FL J. JENKINS, Hon. Treasurer. Bank Chambers, Warrington,
